I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it even when they tell me they don’t see anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're trying to focus on something else, but the thought about your face being crooked just pops into your head without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be trying to do something else, like work at the warehouse or get ready for salsa, and it just comes into my head again that the left side of my face looks crooked. I don’t want to think about it, but then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something you want to have, or do they feel unwanted to you?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to be thinking about my face all the time, but I can’t stop. It just keeps coming back that the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa are noticing, even if they tell me they don’t see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're trying to focus on something else, but the thought about your face being crooked just pops into your head without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be trying to do something else, like work at the warehouse or get ready for salsa, and it just comes into my head again that the left side of my face looks crooked. I don’t want to think about it, but then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something you want to have, or do they feel unwanted to you?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to be thinking about my face all the time, but I can’t stop. It just keeps coming back that the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa are noticing, even if they tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something that you would normally think, or do they feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels out of character for me. I don’t think I used to be this stuck on my face. Now I just can’t stop thinking the left side looks crooked or messed up, and I keep feeling like people at the warehouse or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're trying to focus on something else, but the thought about your face being crooked just pops into your head without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be trying to do something else, like work at the warehouse or get ready for salsa, and it just comes into my head again that the left side of my face looks crooked. I don’t want to think about it, but then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something you want to have, or do they feel unwanted to you?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to be thinking about my face all the time, but I can’t stop. It just keeps coming back that the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa are noticing, even if they tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something that you would normally think, or do they feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels out of character for me. I don’t think I used to be this stuck on my face. Now I just can’t stop thinking the left side looks crooked or messed up, and I keep feeling like people at the warehouse or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re really me. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're trying to focus on something else, but the thought about your face being crooked just pops into your head without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be trying to do something else, like work at the warehouse or get ready for salsa, and it just comes into my head again that the left side of my face looks crooked. I don’t want to think about it, but then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something you want to have, or do they feel unwanted to you?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to be thinking about my face all the time, but I can’t stop. It just keeps coming back that the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa are noticing, even if they tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something that you would normally think, or do they feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels out of character for me. I don’t think I used to be this stuck on my face. Now I just can’t stop thinking the left side looks crooked or messed up, and I keep feeling like people at the warehouse or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re really me. I mean, I know it’s my face I’m thinking about, but I don’t want this to be who I am. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I keep feeling like people at the warehouse or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are forced into your mind, or do you feel like you're generating them yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels like they get forced in, honestly. I’m not trying to sit there and think about it, but it just pops up that the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start thinking everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing, even when they tell me they don’t see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about my face. The left side looks crooked to me, like it’s messed up somehow, and I feel like people at the warehouse or when I go salsa dancing are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything. It’s just stuck in my head all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make this thought about your face go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I keep checking it, mostly in the bathroom mirror at work before I go back out on the inventory floor. I’ll fix my cap over and over, rub at the same spot on my face, like I’m trying to make it look right. Sometimes I get stuck doing it so long that I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think something bad would happen if you didn't check or fix your face like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it feels like if I don’t check it, I’m going to go back out on the inventory floor looking messed up, and people will notice. So I end up in the bathroom mirror fixing my cap, rubbing that same spot on my face, trying to get it right. Sometimes I’m in there so long I’m late getting back, and then my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your appearance that you're worried about when you think your face is crooked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s really just my appearance — mostly that left side of my face. I get stuck thinking it looks crooked or messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face actually looks crooked, or is it just that you feel like it looks crooked to you?\n\nPatient: I mean… to me it actually looks crooked. Like the left side of my face looks messed up, and I can’t stop seeing it. Other people tell me they don’t notice anything, but I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about your face being crooked might be wrong, even a little bit?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe it could be wrong, but it doesn’t feel wrong to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to look in the mirror and not notice the crookedness you're talking about?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it feels almost impossible. When I look in the mirror, my eyes go right to the left side of my face, and I see it as crooked or messed up. Even if other people say they don’t notice anything, I still feel like it’s there and that people at work or salsa night can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the left side of your face looking crooked been affecting your relationships with others or your ability to do your job at the warehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. At the warehouse I’ve been written up because I get behind on my work. I’ll slow down or get delayed because I’m worried people are looking at my face.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve started skipping stuff—soccer, salsa nights, even family gatherings—because I don’t want to deal with people looking at me or taking pictures.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being crooked limited to the left side or do you also worry about other parts of your body?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side of my face. That’s the thing I can’t stop focusing on — like it looks crooked or messed up. I don’t really think about other parts of my body the same way. It’s my face, especially that side, and I feel like people notice it even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks crooked?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess it’s possible, but it doesn’t feel that way to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Even when people tell me they don’t see anything, I still feel like everyone at the warehouse or at salsa night is noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control these thoughts about your face being crooked, or do they feel like they're happening against your will?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like I can control it. It’s like the thought just keeps coming up on its own — that the left side of my face looks crooked or messed up. Even if people tell me they don’t see anything, I still keep thinking everyone is noticing it, like at work or if I go out dancing.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked feel like they are a part of you or do they feel foreign, like they are coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from me, not like some outside voice or anything. It’s my own thought, but I can’t shut it off. I keep thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up, and then I start feeling like people at work or salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are in line with your own beliefs and values, or do they feel like they are contradicting them?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels in line with what I believe, because I really do think the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. I don’t want to be stuck on it like this, but it feels true to me. I keep thinking people at the warehouse or when I go to salsa notice it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your face being crooked ever pop into your head when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it pops in when I’m not trying to think about it. I’ll just be at the warehouse or getting ready to go out, and suddenly I’m thinking the left side of my face looks crooked and messed up. Then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even if they tell me they don’t notice anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have moments where you're trying to focus on something else, but the thought about your face being crooked just pops into your head without you wanting it to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be trying to do something else, like work at the warehouse or get ready for salsa, and it just comes into my head again that the left side of my face looks crooked. I don’t want to think about it, but then I start wondering if everyone can see it, even when they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something you want to have, or do they feel unwanted to you?\n\nPatient: They feel unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to be thinking about my face all the time, but I can’t stop. It just keeps coming back that the left side looks crooked and messed up, and then I’m sure people at work or at salsa are noticing, even if they tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are something that you would normally think, or do they feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it feels out of character for me. I don’t think I used to be this stuck on my face. Now I just can’t stop thinking the left side looks crooked or messed up, and I keep feeling like people at the warehouse or at salsa night are noticing it, even when they say they don’t see anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your face being crooked are a part of who you are, or do they feel like they're not really you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re really me.
